    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your current candidates, ask the one follow-up question that best separates them.\n\n[Current Turn]\n20\n\n[Current Candidates]\nF50.0, F50.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, so… I’m here because I went through this clinic screening for a logistics job, you know, the kind where they want to make sure you’re fit to move around, lift things, be on your feet, that sort of work. And they told me my weight was too low to clear me.\n\nI mean, I was kind of surprised they made such a big issue out of it, because from my point of view I don’t look, you know, “bad” or anything. I look lean. That’s how I’d put it. Like someone who can stay quick on his feet and get through a workday without being slowed down. I’ve done physical work, so I know what it takes, and carrying extra weight doesn’t exactly help with that.\n\nBut they said they couldn’t sign off unless I came here or got evaluated, so… that’s what brings me in.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is currently normal?\n\nPatient: I think it’s normal for me, yeah. I know other people don’t see it that way. My sister keeps telling me I’m too thin and that I’m putting myself in danger, and the doctor at the screening made it sound like it was some major problem, like I’m not safe the way I am.\n\nBut honestly, I think they’re making it bigger than it is. They don’t really understand what I’m trying to do. I’m keeping myself light, sharp, and ready to work. In my line of work, you don’t want to be weighed down. You need to move, lift, get around, stay on your feet. So to me, no, I don’t see my weight as abnormal. I see it as being practical.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would look better if you gained some weight?\n\nPatient: Honestly? No, I don’t think I’d look better. I know that’s probably not the answer you’re looking for, but when I look in the mirror, especially around my stomach and my waist, that’s what I notice first. It’s more that, when I see any softness there, I feel like I’m not the disciplined man I used to be when I was on the warehouse floor, moving all day, keeping up, not letting myself get sloppy. That’s the word that comes to mind, even if other people think I’m being too hard on myself.\n\nSo if I gained weight, I think I’d just be looking at my waist all the time, feeling like I’d let myself go. I know other people might say I’d look healthier or more normal, but to me it would feel like losing control of the one thing I’ve kept in line.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t like the word “afraid,” because it makes it sound like I’m panicking or something, but yes, I think about it a lot.\n\nLike if I eat rice, or bread, or anything greasy, it doesn’t just end when the meal is over. I’ll be thinking, okay, that’s going to sit on me now. That’s going to show up somewhere, probably around my waist. And then the next morning I’ll check—maybe the scale, maybe just how my belt feels—just to make sure I didn’t let myself go from one meal.\n\nI know that probably sounds like I’m making too much out of a little thing, but it’s hard not to think that way. Those foods feel like they stick to me right away. So, yeah… gaining weight is something I try hard to avoid.\n\nDoctor: Does thinking about gaining weight affect what you do on a daily basis?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it does. Probably more than I like to admit. It kind of decides what I let myself eat in a day.\n\nMost days I’ll keep it pretty small—black coffee in the morning, maybe a boiled egg if I feel like I need something, or a little tuna later on. Not a full plate or anything. And I sort of measure it against what I’ve done physically that day. Like if I’m actually moving freight, lifting, walking, being on my feet, then maybe I can justify a little more. But if I’m not on a shift, or if it’s a lighter day, I tell myself, well, I didn’t really earn a real meal today.\n\nI know that probably sounds strict, but in my head it feels logical. If I’m not burning it off, then why put it in? That’s how I think about it. And once that thought is there, it’s hard to just sit down and eat like other people do.\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror, do you see yourself as being underweight?\n\nPatient: No… not really. I mean, I can see that I’m lighter than I used to be, sure. I’m not blind to that. My clothes fit different, my face maybe looks a little sharper, and people keep pointing it out like I haven’t noticed. But when I look in the mirror, I don’t look at myself and think, “Oh, I’m dangerously underweight.” That’s not where my mind goes.\n\nMy sister keeps saying I’m in danger, and doctors have said things like that too, but honestly, part of me feels like they’re making it bigger than it is. They see a number or they see that I’m thinner than other men my age, and they jump to worst-case thinking. But to me, I’m just keeping myself light. That’s how I think of it. Light, sharp, ready to move, ready for work. When you’ve spent years doing physical work, freight, lifting, being on your feet, you get used to knowing what your body feels like when it’s useful. Heavy feels slow to me. Soft feels like I’m not ready.\n\nSo no, when I look in the mirror, I don’t see “underweight” the way everyone else seems to. I see areas I still watch. I see whether I look controlled or not. I know people are worried, but it’s hard for me to see what they’re seeing.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the main reason you restrict what you eat?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t always say it that plainly to myself, like, “I’m doing this because I’m scared of gaining weight,” but that’s basically what it is.\n\nIt’s not just hunger or appetite or being picky. It’s more like certain foods set off this whole chain in my head. If I eat rice, or bread, or something greasy, I can feel myself already thinking, okay, that’s going to sit on me. Like it’s going to turn into weight right away, especially around my middle. And then it doesn’t leave me alone. I’ll be thinking about it later that night, and the next morning I’ll check—maybe get on the scale, maybe just see how my belt feels—like I need proof I didn’t let myself go from one meal.\n\nSo yes, that’s a big reason I restrict. Maybe the main reason. Because if I don’t eat those things, I don’t have to deal with that panic afterward, or that checking, or that feeling that I messed up and now I have to watch myself even harder.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel fat even if you were at a normal weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I probably would. That’s the hard thing to explain, because “normal weight” sounds like it should feel safe, like it should settle the question. But I don’t think my mind works that way when it comes to my body.\n\nIf I looked in the mirror and saw more around my stomach or my waist, even if someone else told me, “No, that’s normal, that’s healthy,” I don’t think I’d feel normal. I’d feel… soft, I guess. Like I’d lost something. Especially around my middle. That’s the place I check without even meaning to sometimes. If my waist doesn’t look tight, or if my stomach doesn’t look flat enough, my head goes straight to, you’re slipping.\n\nAnd it’s tied up with how I see myself as a man, honestly. I spent so many years on the warehouse floor, moving freight, lifting, staying active, being the guy who could handle the physical work. In my head, being smaller and tight-looking means I’m still disciplined, still that guy. If I’m not that way, if I look heavier or looser, then it feels like I’m not the disciplined man I used to be. Even if the number was supposedly normal, I think I’d still feel fat if I didn’t look controlled.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like gaining weight would mean you're losing control over your life or body?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I think that’s what it feels like. Maybe not “my whole life” in every way, but definitely my body, and then that spills into how I feel about myself.\n\nWhen I look in the mirror, it’s mostly my stomach and waist I go to first. I don’t even know if I decide to do it anymore—it’s just where my eyes land. And if I’m not smaller there, if I don’t look tight, then it feels like I’m losing the thing that made me feel disciplined. Like I’m not the same man I used to be when I was on the warehouse floor, moving all day, lifting, keeping up, being useful.\n\nI know that might sound strange because people probably just think weight is weight. But to me, gaining doesn’t feel neutral. It feels like getting softer, like letting myself slide. Like I’m not keeping a grip on myself. And once I start thinking that, it’s hard to separate my body from everything else. It becomes, if I can’t control this, then what does that say about me?\n\nSo yes… gaining weight would feel like losing control. Not just looking different, but becoming someone less disciplined, less capable, less like the man I’ve always tried to be.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is the main thing that determines your self-worth?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I don’t like admitting it that way, because it sounds shallow or ridiculous when I hear it said out loud. Like my whole worth as a person comes down to my waistline. But… if I’m being honest, a lot of the time it does feel that way.\n\nWhen I look in the mirror, I don’t really see the whole person first. I go straight to my stomach and my waist. I’m checking whether I look smaller, whether I look tight, whether I still look like I’ve got control over myself. And if I don’t see that—if I look softer there, or bigger, or just not as lean as I think I should—then it hits me hard. It’s not just, “Oh, I gained weight.” It turns into, “You’re not disciplined anymore. You’re not the man you used to be.”\n\nI think about when I was working on the warehouse floor, moving all day, lifting things, keeping pace, being useful that way. In my head, that version of me was disciplined and capable. So when my body doesn’t look like that—especially around the middle—it feels like I’ve lost that part of myself. Like I’m less respectable somehow, even if nobody else is saying that.\n\nSo yes… I think my weight and shape matter way too much in how I judge myself. Maybe they’re not the only thing, but they become the first thing. And if I don’t feel right in my body, especially if I don’t look smaller and tight-looking, it’s hard for me to feel like I’m worth much.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is because you believe you would look unattractive at a higher weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, unattractive is part of it, I guess, but it’s not just like, “Oh, I won’t look good.” It feels more immediate than that, more like I’ve done something wrong and it’s going to show on me right away.\n\nLike if I eat rice or bread, or something greasy, I can’t just eat it and move on. My head starts going almost immediately—like it’s going to sit on me, like I’m going to wake up and it’ll be there on my stomach or around my waist. I know that probably sounds dramatic, because one meal shouldn’t suddenly change your whole body, but that’s where my mind goes. It feels like it will stick to me.\n\nAnd then the next morning I’ll want to check. I’ll check the scale, or I’ll see how my belt feels, like whether it’s tighter or whether I let myself go somehow. That phrase is in my head a lot—“letting myself go.” So yes, maybe it’s about looking unattractive at a higher weight, but more than that it’s this fear that if I eat the wrong thing, especially bread or rice or greasy food, I’ve lost control and it’ll show on me right away.\n\nDoctor: Do you think gaining weight would change the way you feel about your body shape?\n\nPatient: Yes… definitely. Gaining weight would change how I feel about my shape. I don’t think I’d be able to just see it as my body changing in a normal way. I’d go right to judging it.\n\nFor me it’s always my stomach and waist first. That’s where I look in the mirror before anything else. If I look smaller there, tighter, like there’s control, then I feel… not good exactly, but more like myself. More like I’m still disciplined. But if I looked bigger there, or softer, I think it would really mess with how I see myself.\n\nIt’s hard to explain without sounding like I’m making too much of it, but when I was on the warehouse floor, moving all day and keeping up, I had this picture of myself as a disciplined man. Useful. Strong enough. In control of myself. And now when I look at my body, especially around the middle, that’s what I’m measuring against. So if gaining weight changed that shape, it wouldn’t just feel like my body looked different. It would feel like proof that I’m not that man anymore.\n\nSo yes, it would change how I feel about my body shape a lot. Especially if I didn’t look smaller and tight-looking. That’s when I start feeling like I’ve slipped, like I’m not who I’m supposed to be.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is mostly about how you would look or how you would feel about yourself?\n\nPatient: I think… it’s both, but if I’m really trying to be honest, it’s more about how I would feel about myself. The looking part is what I focus on first, because that’s what I can see. I look in the mirror and my eyes go straight to my stomach and my waist, like I’m inspecting myself. But underneath that, it’s not just “Do I look good?” It’s more like, “Am I still disciplined? Like I had control over myself.\n\nSo if I gained weight, especially around my middle, it would feel like more than appearance. It would feel like evidence that I’ve lost something in myself. Like I’m not that man anymore. That’s the part that scares me most, I think.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is the only thing that determines whether you have a good body shape?\n\nPatient: No… not the only thing, I don’t think. I mean, weight is the number I get stuck on because it feels simple, like something I can measure and know where I stand. But when I’m actually looking at myself, it’s not just the number. It’s the shape. It’s my stomach, my waist, whether I look smaller there, whether I look tight-looking or like I’ve gone soft.\n\nThat’s where my mind goes immediately in the mirror. I can look at the rest of me, but I don’t really see it first. I check the middle. It’s like… if I’m not smaller and tight-looking there, then I’m not disciplined. I’m not the man I used to be when I was working on the warehouse floor, moving all day, doing physical work, keeping up.\n\nSo no, weight isn’t the only thing. But it all kind of connects in my head. The weight, the waist, the stomach, the shape — it all becomes proof of whether I’m still in control of myself. And if I don’t see that controlled shape, I feel like I’ve slipped somehow. Like I’m not who I’m supposed to be.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still want to lose weight or maintain your current weight even if a doctor told you it was healthy?\n\nPatient: I think I would still want to maintain it, yes… maybe even lose a little, if I’m being completely honest. Even if a doctor said, “This is healthy,” I don’t know that it would quiet the part of my head that worries about it.\n\nBecause healthy is one thing, but what I feel in my body is another. Like if I eat rice, or bread, or something greasy, I can’t just think, “That was a normal meal.” My mind goes right to, “That’s going to sit on me.” Like it’s going to show up right away on my stomach or my waist. And then the next morning I’m checking. I’ll look at the scale, or I’ll check my belt, just to make sure I didn’t let myself go overnight.\n\nI know that probably sounds extreme, because logically one meal shouldn’t change everything. But it feels like it could. It feels like if I don’t keep watching it, I’ll lose control before I realize it. So even if a doctor told me the number was healthy, I think I’d still be worried about staying there. I’d still be checking to make sure it hadn’t changed.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to control your weight if you didn't care about how your body looks?\n\nPatient: I think… yes. I wish I could say no, because that would make it sound simpler, like if I didn’t care about appearance then I’d be free of it. But I don’t think it’s only about how I look to other people, or even just what I see in the mirror.\n\nWhen I look at myself, especially my stomach and my waist, it turns into this whole judgment of who I am. If I’m not smaller there, if I don’t look tight-looking, I don’t just think, “Oh, I don’t like my body.” I think, “You’re slipping. You’re not disciplined. You’re not the man you used to be.”\n\nAnd that’s the part that’s hard to separate from looks. Back when I was on the warehouse floor, moving all day, doing physical work, I felt like my body proved something about me. Like I could keep up, like I had control, like I was useful. So now when I see my middle looking different — or even think it might look different — it feels like I’m losing that version of myself.\n\nSo even if I somehow didn’t care about looking good, I think I’d still feel like I had to control my weight, because in my head it’s tied to being disciplined. It’s tied to whether I still feel like myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to restrict your food intake if you didn't believe it affected your self-worth?\n\nPatient: I think… I’d like to say no, that if it didn’t mean anything about me as a person then I’d just eat normally. But I’m not sure it would stop that easily, because restricting has become such a habit, almost like a rule I follow before I’ve even thought it through.\n\nMost days I keep it very small. Black coffee, maybe a boiled egg if I feel like I need something, or a little tuna. Something plain, something I can sort of count in my head and not feel like I’ve gone too far. And then if I’m not moving freight on a shift, if I’m not doing heavy work that day, I tell myself I don’t really need a real meal anyway. Like, “What did you do to earn that?” Not even always in a dramatic way — it’s just the thought that comes up.\n\nSo even if you took away the self-worth part, I think I’d still be telling myself, “You don’t need much today.” Especially on days I’m not physically active. It feels safer to keep it low. Like if I don’t eat much, at least I haven’t made a mistake.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be trying to lose weight if you weren't afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I think the fear is a big part of it. Like it’s going to turn into weight overnight, or show up around my stomach or my beltline. And then the next morning I’m checking — the scale, or how my belt feels — just to make sure I didn’t let myself go. Even if part of me knows that sounds unreasonable, the worry is still there.\n\nSo if that fear wasn’t there at all… maybe I wouldn’t be trying to lose weight in the same way. But because it is there, it feels like I have to stay ahead of it. Like if I’m not careful, it’ll happen before I catch it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is more about losing control over your body or about losing control in other areas of your life?\n\nPatient: I think it’s both, but if I’m being honest, it starts with my body. Like if I’m not careful, it’ll happen before I catch it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is more about losing control over your body or about losing control in other areas of your life?\n\nPatient: I think it’s both, but if I’m being honest, it starts with my body. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think it probably is, at least some of the time. It’s hard for me to say that because when I’m looking at myself, it doesn’t feel distorted. It feels like I’m just seeing what’s there.

But when I look in the mirror, especially around my stomach and my waist, that’s where my eyes go right away. I don’t look at myself as a whole person. I go straight to whether my stomach looks flat enough, whether my waist looks tight enough, whether there’s any softness there. And if I don’t look smaller or tight-looking, I get this feeling like… like I’m not the same disciplined man I used to be.

I know that sounds tied up with pride, maybe. But when I was working the warehouse floor, moving freight, doing hard physical work, I had this idea of myself as controlled and capable. Solid. And now if my body doesn’t look a certain way, I read it like evidence that I’ve slipped. Like I’ve gotten soft, not just physically but as a person.

So yes, I guess my mind probably does twist it. Because I’m not just seeing weight or shape. I’m seeing whether I’m disciplined enough, whether I still measure up to who I think I’m supposed to be.

I look lean. That’s how I’d put it. Like someone who can stay quick on his feet and get through a workday without being slowed down. My sister keeps telling me I’m too thin and that I’m putting myself in danger, and the doctor at the screening made it sound like it was some major problem, like I’m not safe the way I am.\n\nBut honestly, I think they’re making it bigger than it is. I’ll be thinking, okay, that’s going to sit on me now. That’s going to show up somewhere, probably around my waist. Those foods feel like they stick to me right away. It kind of decides what I let myself eat in a day.\n\nMost days I’ll keep it pretty small—black coffee in the morning, maybe a boiled egg if I feel like I need something, or a little tuna later on. Not a full plate or anything. And I sort of measure it against what I’ve done physically that day. Like if I’m actually moving freight, lifting, walking, being on my feet, then maybe I can justify a little more. But if I’m not on a shift, or if it’s a lighter day, I tell myself, well, I didn’t really earn a real meal today.\n\nI know that probably sounds strict, but in my head it feels logical. I mean, I can see that I’m lighter than I used to be, sure. I’m not blind to that. My clothes fit different, my face maybe looks a little sharper, and people keep pointing it out like I haven’t noticed. But when I look in the mirror, I don’t look at myself and think, “Oh, I’m dangerously underweight.” That’s not where my mind goes.\n\nMy sister keeps saying I’m in danger, and doctors have said things like that too, but honestly, part of me feels like they’re making it bigger than it is. They see a number or they see that I’m thinner than other men my age, and they jump to worst-case thinking. But to me, I’m just keeping myself light. That’s how I think of it. Light, sharp, ready to move, ready for work. When you’ve spent years doing physical work, freight, lifting, being on your feet, you get used to knowing what your body feels like when it’s useful. Heavy feels slow to me. Soft feels like I’m not ready.\n\nSo no, when I look in the mirror, I don’t see “underweight” the way everyone else seems to. I see areas I still watch. I see whether I look controlled or not. I know people are worried, but it’s hard for me to see what they’re seeing.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the main reason you restrict what you eat?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s more like certain foods set off this whole chain in my head. If I eat rice, or bread, or something greasy, I can feel myself already thinking, okay, that’s going to sit on me. Like it’s going to turn into weight right away, especially around my middle. And then it doesn’t leave me alone. I’ll be thinking about it later that night, and the next morning I’ll check—maybe get on the scale, maybe just see how my belt feels—like I need proof I didn’t let myself go from one meal.\n\nSo yes, that’s a big reason I restrict. Maybe the main reason. Because if I don’t eat those things, I don’t have to deal with that panic afterward, or that checking, or that feeling that I messed up and now I have to watch myself even harder.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel fat even if you were at a normal weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I probably would. That’s the hard thing to explain, because “normal weight” sounds like it should feel safe, like it should settle the question. But I don’t think my mind works that way when it comes to my body.\n\nIf I looked in the mirror and saw more around my stomach or my waist, even if someone else told me, “No, that’s normal, that’s healthy,” I don’t think I’d feel normal. I’d feel… soft, I guess. Like I’d lost something. Especially around my middle. That’s the place I check without even meaning to sometimes. If my waist doesn’t look tight, or if my stomach doesn’t look flat enough, my head goes straight to, you’re slipping.\n\nAnd it’s tied up with how I see myself as a man, honestly. In my head, being smaller and tight-looking means I’m still disciplined, still that guy. If I’m not that way, if I look heavier or looser, then it feels like I’m not the disciplined man I used to be. Even if the number was supposedly normal, I think I’d still feel fat if I didn’t look controlled.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like gaining weight would mean you're losing control over your life or body?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I think that’s what it feels like. Maybe not “my whole life” in every way, but definitely my body, and then that spills into how I feel about myself.\n\nWhen I look in the mirror, it’s mostly my stomach and waist I go to first. I don’t even know if I decide to do it anymore—it’s just where my eyes land. And if I’m not smaller there, if I don’t look tight, then it feels like I’m losing the thing that made me feel disciplined. Like I’m not the same man I used to be when I was on the warehouse floor, moving all day, lifting, keeping up, being useful.\n\nI know that might sound strange because people probably just think weight is weight. But to me, gaining doesn’t feel neutral. It feels like getting softer, like letting myself slide. Like I’m not keeping a grip on myself. And once I start thinking that, it’s hard to separate my body from everything else. It becomes, if I can’t control this, then what does that say about me?\n\nSo yes… gaining weight would feel like losing control. Not just looking different, but becoming someone less disciplined, less capable, less like the man I’ve always tried to be.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is the main thing that determines your self-worth?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I don’t like admitting it that way, because it sounds shallow or ridiculous when I hear it said out loud. Like my whole worth as a person comes down to my waistline. But… if I’m being honest, a lot of the time it does feel that way.\n\nWhen I look in the mirror, I don’t really see the whole person first. But it feels like it could. It feels like if I don’t keep watching it, I’ll lose control before I realize it. So even if a doctor told me the number was healthy, I think I’d still be worried about staying there. I’d still be checking to make sure it hadn’t changed.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to control your weight if you didn't care about how your body looks?\n\nPatient: I think… yes. Black coffee, maybe a boiled egg if I feel like I need something, or a little tuna. Something plain, something I can sort of count in my head and not feel like I’ve gone too far. And then if I’m not moving freight on a shift, if I’m not doing heavy work that day, I tell myself I don’t really need a real meal anyway. Like, “What did you do to earn that?” Not even always in a dramatic way — it’s just the thought that comes up.\n\nSo even if you took away the self-worth part, I think I’d still be telling myself, “You don’t need much today.” Especially on days I’m not physically active. It feels safer to keep it low. Like if I don’t eat much, at least I haven’t made a mistake.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be trying to lose weight if you weren't afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I think the fear is a big part of it. Maybe the biggest part, honestly. If I truly wasn’t afraid of gaining weight, then maybe I wouldn’t be pushing so hard to lose. But it’s hard for me to even imagine not having that fear there, because it comes up so automatically.\n\nLike, if I eat rice or bread or something greasy, I don’t just think, “Okay, I ate that.” I start thinking it’s going to sit on me right away. Like it’s going to turn into weight overnight, or show up around my stomach or my beltline. And then the next morning I’m checking — the scale, or how my belt feels — just to make sure I didn’t let myself go. Even if part of me knows that sounds unreasonable, the worry is still there.\n\nSo if that fear wasn’t there at all… maybe I wouldn’t be trying to lose weight in the same way. But because it is there, it feels like I have to stay ahead of it. Like if I’m not careful, it’ll happen before I catch it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is more about losing control over your body or about losing control in other areas of your life?\n\nPatient: I think it’s both, but if I’m being honest, it starts with my body. That’s where I feel it first. Like if I eat rice, or bread, or anything greasy, I can’t just let it be a meal and move on. My mind goes straight to, “That’s going to sit on me.” Like it’s going to show up right away, almost as if by the next morning I’ll be able to see it or feel it.\n\nAnd then I’ll check. I’ll get on the scale, or I’ll put my belt on and pay attention to whether it feels different. Not in a casual way — more like I’m trying to catch it before it gets away from me. Like I need proof that I didn’t let myself go.\n\nSo yes, it’s about control over my body, definitely. But I think when I feel that panic, it doesn’t stay only physical. It turns into this bigger feeling that if I can’t control that, then maybe I’m losing control in general. Like one greasy meal or too much bread means I’ve failed at holding the line. It sounds ridiculous when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels very real.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is distorted in your mind compared to how it actually is?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it probably is, at least some of the time. It’s hard for me to say that because when I’m looking at myself, it doesn’t feel distorted. It feels like I’m just seeing what’s there.\n\nBut when I look in the mirror, especially around my stomach and my waist, that’s where my eyes go right away. I don’t look at myself as a whole person. I go straight to whether my stomach looks flat enough, whether my waist looks tight enough, whether there’s any softness there. And if I don’t look smaller or tight-looking, I get this feeling like… like I’m not the same disciplined man I used to be.\n\nI know that sounds tied up with pride, maybe. But when I was working the warehouse floor, moving freight, doing hard physical work, I had this idea of myself as controlled and capable. Solid. And now if my body doesn’t look a certain way, I read it like evidence that I’ve slipped. Like I’ve gotten soft, not just physically but as a person.\n\nSo yes, I guess my mind probably does twist it. Because I’m not just seeing weight or shape. I’m seeing whether I’m disciplined enough, whether I still measure up to who I think I’m supposed to be.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
